Source selection shapes the strength of cultural transmission pathways Authors

To fix (2)

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 1 imprecise p value out of 58 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
p > .072 E1 Same, extended to age and parents Waves 1-6 s ~ age_c × transmission × age_group + (1 ｜ppt) 1,183 participants, 5,039 trials Age × horizontal b = 0.019, p = .030 Main text; Fig 2; Table S4 E1 Source choice as a function of age Waves 7-10 Multinomial logistic, transmission ~ age, fitted separately for children and parents Children 834 (4,169 trials); parents 829 (4,145 trials) Vertical preferred in both groups (children, oblique vs. vertical OR = 0.25; parents OR = 0.11); no significant age effects (all p > .072)

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To check (3)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 32 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of misinterpretations of non-significant results is high, see:

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

Code Comments

Best programming practice is to add comments to code, to explain what the code does (to yourself in the future, or peers who want to re-use your code). All your code files had comments.

Missing Files

All files loaded in the code were present in the repository.

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 2 code files, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

Packages / Dependencies

The code files load 12 distinct packages/libraries: dplyr, emmeans, ggforce, ggplot2, lme4, lmerTest, mclogit, MuMIn, nnet, patchwork, tibble, tidyr. These are the names found in the source (no version information is available from static analysis).

Reproducible Environment

No renv.lock file, sessionInfo()/session_info() record, or groundhog/checkpoint date-pin call was found anywhere in the repository. Without one of these, the exact R and package versions used for the analysis are not recoverable, and package versions may drift between when this analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt. Consider depositing an renv.lock (via the renv package), a sessionInfo() text dump, or a groundhog/checkpoint date-pin alongside the code.

All R-type code files (.R, .Rmd, .qmd) could be read in. There were no parsing issues.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

A script uses something it does not create or load itself.

  • _setup.R: repo_root

Usually the object was still in your R session from earlier work, or another script made it.

  1. Restart R (in RStudio: Session > Restart R).
  2. Run each script from top to bottom, and fix what is missing.
  3. If a script needs results of another, save them to a file in the first script and read them in the second.
  4. Load every package with library() at the top.

Metacheck can’t automatically establish what caused the error, or how it should be improved.

>               sprintf("= %.3f", h1_cor$p.value))
> rho_label <- sprintf("italic(r)[s] == %.2f~', '*italic(p)~'%s'",
>                      h1_cor$estimate, p_a)
> panel_A <- ggplot(dyad_S_all, aes(x = caregiver, y = child)) +
>   geom_point(alpha = 0.25, size = 1.5, color = "#999999") +
>   geom_smooth(method = "lm", se = TRUE,
>               color = "#2166AC", fill = "#2166AC", alpha = 0.15,
>               linewidth = 0.9) +
>   annotate("text", x = 0.98, y = 0.97,
>            label = rho_label,
>            parse = TRUE, hjust = 1, vjust = 1, size = 3.8) +
>   scale_x_continuous(limits = c(0, 1), breaks = seq(0, 1, 0.25)) +

> selected <- dyad_S_all
> test <- suppressWarnings(cor.test(selected$caregiver, selected$child, method="spearman"))
> n_pairs <- nrow(selected)
> rho <- unname(test$estimate)
> stopifnot(n_pairs > 3L, is.finite(rho), abs(rho) <= 1,
>           all(is.finite(selected$caregiver)), all(is.finite(selected$child)))
> conf_level <- 0.95
> z_margin <- qnorm((1 + conf_level) / 2) / sqrt(n_pairs - 3)
> rho_ci <- tanh(atanh(rho) + c(-1, 1) * z_margin)
> write.csv(data.frame(
>   n_families = n_pairs,
>   rho = rho,
>   p_value = test$p.value,
>   conf_low = rho_ci[1],
>   conf_high = rho_ci[2],
>   conf_level = conf_level,
>   ci_method = "Fisher-z approximation for Spearman rho"

>     age_group == "Child"
>   ) %>%

>     transmission = factor(transmission, levels = c("Vertical", "Oblique", "Horizontal"))
>   )

> pooled_age_mean <- mean(h5_trials_all$age_x, na.rm = TRUE)
> h5_trials_all <- h5_trials_all %>%
>   mutate(age_c = age_x - pooled_age_mean)

>   s_adj ~ age_c * transmission + (1 | ppt),
>   data = h5_trials_all,
>   REML = FALSE
> )

>       age_group == "adult" & socialSource == 1 ~ own_child_age,
>       age_group == "adult" & socialSource == 3 ~ own_child_age,
>       TRUE ~ NA_real_
>     )
>   )
> choice_data <- choice_data %>%
>   mutate(
>     transmission = case_when(
>       socialSource == 1 ~ "vertical",
>       socialSource == 2 ~ "horizontal",
>       socialSource == 3 ~ "oblique",
>       TRUE ~ NA_character_
>     )
>   )
> h5_trials_w7_8 <- choice_data %>%
>   mutate(
>     age_group = factor(
>       if_else(age_group == "child", "Child", "Adult"),
>       levels = c("Child", "Adult")
>     ),
>     transmission = case_when(
>       age_group == "Child" & socialSource == 1 ~ "Vertical",
>       age_group == "Child" & socialSource == 2 ~ "Horizontal",
>       age_group == "Child" & socialSource == 3 ~ "Oblique",
>       age_group == "Adult" & socialSource == 1 ~ "Vertical",
>       age_group == "Adult" & socialSource == 3 ~ "Oblique",
>       TRUE ~ NA_character_
>     ),
>     age_x = source_child_age
>   ) %>%
>   filter(
>     !is.na(transmission),
>     !is.na(age_x),
>     age_x >= 6,
>     age_x <= 18
>   ) %>%
>   mutate(
>     transmission = factor(
>       transmission,
>       levels = c("Vertical", "Oblique", "Horizontal")
>     )
>   )

>   filter(age_group == "Child")
> h5_trials_w7_8 <- h5_trials_w7_8 %>%
>   mutate(age_c = age_x - mean(age_x, na.rm = TRUE))

>   s_adj ~ age_c * transmission + (1 | ppt),
>   data = h5_trials_w7_8,
>   REML = FALSE
> )
> age_means <- h5_trials_w7_8 %>%
>   group_by(age_x, transmission) %>%
>   summarise(
>     mean_S_age = mean(s_adj, na.rm = TRUE),
>     n = n_distinct(ppt),
>     .groups = "drop"
>   )
> age_seq <- seq(6, 18, length.out = 300)

>   transmission = levels(h5_trials_w7_8$transmission)
> ) %>%
>   as_tibble() %>%
>   mutate(
>     transmission = factor(transmission, levels = levels(h5_trials_w7_8$transmission)),
>     age_c = age_x - mean(h5_trials_w7_8$age_x, na.rm = TRUE)
>   )
> pred_grid$fit <- predict(h5_model_lmer_w7_8, newdata = pred_grid, re.form = NA)
> X <- model.matrix(delete.response(terms(h5_model_lmer_w7_8)), pred_grid)
> beta <- fixef(h5_model_lmer_w7_8)
> V <- vcov(h5_model_lmer_w7_8)
> X <- X[, names(beta), drop = FALSE]
> pred_grid$se  <- sqrt(diag(X %*% V %*% t(X)))
> pred_grid$lwr <- pred_grid$fit - 1.96 * pred_grid$se
> pred_grid$upr <- pred_grid$fit + 1.96 * pred_grid$se
> line_N <- h5_trials_w7_8 %>%
>   group_by(transmission) %>%
>   summarise(N = n_distinct(ppt), .groups = "drop") %>%

> age_means_comb <- age_means %>%

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

Parent-child participants completed a perceptual judgment task on separate tablet computers (Fig. 2: 685 children and 669 parents; Methods). on separate tablet computers.

Participants were recruited at NEMO Science Museum in Amsterdam, The Netherlands, and invited to complete a brief experiment.

All participants were fluent in Dutch or English.

Participants were instructed not to communicate with each other during the session.

Participants completed the task privately on their own tablet.

Participants had to move the slider before they could continue.

Participants were instructed to estimate as accurately as possible.

In wave 2, participants completed five additional trials in which the source of the social information was not identified as a child or adult.

Estimates from a linear mixed-effects model predicting social information use (s) from whether participants had selected the observed source, receiver, age, and transmission pathway, with participant as a random intercept, pooling data from Experiments 1 and 2.

Ethics approval statement:

The study was approved by the Ethics Review Board of the University of Amsterdam (2021-DP-13852).

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 66 columns across 1 data file.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 1 data file could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.
