How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited (currently, a warning is provided regardless of whether the replication/reproduction study is already cited).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Human culture depends on people learning from one another. Cultural evolutionary theory distinguishes three transmission pathways: learning from parents (vertical), non-parental adults (oblique), and peers (horizontal), which differ in how rapidly information spreads within and across generations. During middle childhood, children's social worlds expand beyond the family, creating opportunities for all three pathways to operate simultaneously, yet their relative strength has rarely been compared directly. Using 1,262 dyads of 6-13 year-olds and their parents, we examined the relative strength of vertical, oblique, and horizontal transmission in two complementary behavioral experiments. Participants either received information from, or chose between, their own parent or child, an unrelated adult or child, and a peer while completing an estimation task. This design allowed us to separate two components of cultural transmission: whom people choose to learn from and how strongly they use information once it has been acquired. Vertical transmission emerged as the strongest pathway. This advantage was driven primarily by source selection, as children and parents overwhelmingly selected one another as information sources, with parents learning from their own child providing evidence for reverse vertical transmission. Once information had been acquired, however, information weighting differed little between vertical, oblique, and horizontal transmission. Instead, developmental differences dominated: social information use declined markedly across middle childhood and remained consistently higher in children than in parents. Together, these findings suggest that the relative strength of cultural transmission pathways is determined primarily by source selection rather than information weighting.

Introduction

Humans acquire much of what they know from other people (1) (2) (3). Parents pass on knowledge and skills to their children, children learn from teachers and other adults, and peers learn from one another. Through such social learning, knowledge, beliefs, and behaviors that make up human culture spread between individuals and across generations (4) (5) (6) (7) (8). Which sources people learn from therefore shapes both what individuals come to know and how information moves through populations. Information transmitted primarily from parents to children can spread only as new generations acquire it, whereas information acquired from peers or unrelated adults can diffuse more rapidly among individuals within the same generation (4, 5, 9). Despite the importance of these different routes for cultural stability and change, little is known about their relative strength when they operate side by side, or about the individual-level processes that make one route more influential than another.

Cultural evolutionary theory formalizes these routes as vertical transmission from parent to offspring, oblique transmission from unrelated adults to younger generations, and horizontal transmission between members of the same generation (4, 5, 7, (10) (11) (12) (13) (14) (15). These transmission pathways provide a powerful abstraction for studying cultural dynamics, but their effective strength emerges from the behavior of individual learners. A pathway can be influential because people preferentially turn to that source for information, because they place greater weight on its information once acquired, or both. Moreover, although transmission across generations is typically considered from older to younger individuals, information may also flow in the opposite direction, with children serving as sources for adults (16) (17) (18). Here, we provide a controlled experimental comparison of vertical, oblique, and horizontal transmission and separate these two behavioral components: whom people choose to learn from and how strongly they use the information they acquire.

As children develop, the range of people from whom they can acquire information expands. Early in childhood, parents are especially available and serve as primary sources of socially transmitted information (19) (20) (21). As children's social worlds broaden, interactions with peers and unrelated adults become increasingly common (16, 19, (22) (23) (24) (25) (26) (27). During middle childhood (approximately 6-13 years), these expanding social environments create opportunities for vertical, oblique, and horizontal transmission to operate alongside one another (17, 19, 24, 28, 29). This developmental transition coincides with increasing cognitive capacities to evaluatehttps://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?O9dwJb comparehttps://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?ZanYOz and selectively use information from different sources (30) (31) (32) (33) , while children's growing knowledge and experience may reduce their reliance on social information (34, 35) , and make them increasingly useful sources of information for others (16, 36). Middle childhood therefore provides a particularly informative period for examining both the pathways through which children acquire information and their role in transmitting it.

Despite extensive interest in social learning, developmental research has primarily examined changes in children's sensitivity to different social learning cues, such as expertise, familiarity, or age (31-33, 35, 37-43). Some experiments have directly compared influence from peers and adults, but these examined only a subset of transmission pathways, often in adolescents rather than across middle childhood (31, 44, 45). Field studies have taken a broader approach, estimating or reconstructing vertical, oblique, and horizontal transmission from reported learning relationships, cultural similarity, or social networks (46) (47) (48) (49). However, the relative strength of the three pathways is difficult to infer by comparing across studies because they differ in tasks, populations, and the kinds of information that is transmitted, ranging from social influence on decisions to the transmission of skills, technologies, and factual knowledge (25, 38, (50) (51) (52) (53). Direct experimental comparison of all three pathways additionally requires multiple generations to participate within the same controlled setting, which is logistically difficult to organize. Taken together, these limitations have left the relative strength of the major transmission pathways, and how it changes over development, largely unresolved.

We address these issues by recruiting 1,262 parent-child dyads with children aged 6-13 years in a large science museum, who completed an estimation task in which they could adjust their initial estimates after observing social information (Fig. 1). This task provides a reliable measure of social information use, with demonstrated test-retest reliability, and has been successfully applied across a wide range of ages and cultural settings, including children as young as six (35, 45, 54). We conducted two experiments that manipulated the source of social information, allowing us to operationalize vertical, oblique, and horizontal transmission within a shared behavioral framework and quantify their relative strength directly. Across experiments, participants either received or selected social information from their parent or child (vertical), an unrelated adult or child (oblique), or a same-generation other (horizontal). Together, the two experiments characterize social transmission along two complementary dimensions (whom individuals choose to learn from and how strongly they use social information once acquiredhttps://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?mVZRQO while allowing us to compare transmission both within and across generations, including from children to parents. 55 )). On each trial, participants viewed an image containing 50-100 animals for 6 seconds and entered a first estimate (E₁) by positioning a slider. They then observed the estimate of another participant (X), displayed together with their own first estimate (E₁) on the slider, before entering a second estimate (E₂). In Experiment 1, the social information source was assigned; in Experiment 2, participants chose their source. B. Social information use was quantified as s = (E₂ -E₁) / (X -E₁), capturing the relative extent of moving one's estimate toward the social information on a given trial. Each participant's mean adjustment across trials is denoted S. C. The three transmission pathways. In the vertical pathway, children received the estimate of their own parent (SV), while parents received that of their child (SRV, where R in the subscript denotes "reverse"). In the oblique pathway, children received an estimate from an unrelated adult (SO); parents received an estimate from an unrelated child (SRO). In the horizontal pathway, participants received the estimate from another individual of the same generation (SH).

We formulated predictions about both components of transmission. First, for how strongly social information is used, we predicted that children would rely more heavily on it than adults, and that this reliance would decline across childhood and adolescence as individuals accumulate their own knowledge and experience. Given the prominent role of parents as information sources early in development, we further predicted that children would weigh information from parents more strongly than information from unrelated adults or peers, particularly at younger ages. Second, for whom individuals choose to learn from, we predicted source preferences to shift across middle childhood, from parents toward peers and unrelated adults as children's social environments expand beyond the family. Finally, our design allowed us to test for intergenerational resemblance in social information use and to look for evidence consistent with reverse transmission, whereby younger generations contribute adaptively relevant information to older generations (16, 36). For preregistration, see https://osf.io/9xn87; for an overview of preregistered and exploratory analyses reported in this paper, see Table S1.

Results

Social information use declines across development.

In Experiment 1 we controlled the source of social information to isolate information use by assigning participants to receive estimates from either their own parent or child (vertical), an unrelated adult or child (oblique), or a peer (horizontal; Fig. 1). Parent-child participants completed a perceptual judgment task on separate tablet computers (Fig. 2: 685 children and 669 parents; Methods). on separate tablet computers. We quantified participants' social information use as their degree of adjustment toward social information (Fig. 1B).

Fig 2. Social information use across transmission pathways (Experiment 1).

Mean social information use (S) plotted against child age for children (red) and parents (blue). Panels show the three transmission pathways: A, vertical (parent-child); B, oblique (unrelated adult-child); C, horizontal (peerpeer). Symbols represent per-age means, with symbol size proportional to sample size. Lines show linear mixed-effects model predictions with 95% confidence intervals. The x-axis corresponds to the age of the child in each dyad; parent data are plotted at their child's age, with unrelated child sources age-matched accordingly (Methods). The axis is truncated at age 13 for legibility; data from sessions with older children (n=16) are retained in all regression models. The dotted blue line in C indicates adults' mean social information use when receiving estimates from another adult (horizontal transmission), for which child-age is not applicable. Slight differences in total sample size between parents and children reflect exclusions for adjustments out of the range0 ≤ s ≤ 1 (Methods).

Across all three pathways, children's social information use declined with age (Fig. 2, red lines; Table S2; β = -0.017, SE = 0.004, t(647) = -4.51, p < .001, 95% CI [-0.024, -0.010]). Averaged across age, children's social information use was 0.369 (95% CI [0.355, 0.382]) and did not differ between transmission pathways (vertical vs. oblique: z = 0.46, p = 1.000; vertical vs. horizontal: z = 1.90, p = .173; Table S3). Likewise, we did not find evidence that children relied more strongly on an unrelated adult than on a peer (β = -0.014, p = .551; Table S1). However, the age-related decline was slightly weaker for horizontal transmission (Table S4; age × horizontal: β = 0.019, SE = 0.009, t(1,194) = 2.17, p = .030, 95% CI [0.002, 0.035]). Children relied substantially more on social information than parents overall (β = 0.185, SE = 0.010, t(1,309) = 19.08, p < .001, 95% CI [0.166, 0.204]).

Among parents, mean social information use was 0.184 (95% CI [0.171, 0.198]), roughly half that of children. The amount of social information use did not differ between transmission pathways or vary with child age (vertical vs. oblique: z = 1.84, p = .197; vertical vs. horizontal: z = 0.82, p = 1.000; Table S3-4).

Together, these results show that who was learning mattered far more for social information use than whom they were learning from. Children relied substantially more on social information than parents, and this reliance declined across childhood, whereas information from parents, unrelated adults, and peers was weighted similarly.

Children and parents preferentially selected each other as information sources.

Experiment 2 examined source selection by allowing participants to choose whose estimate they wished to observe on each trial before deciding how strongly to use that information (Fig. 1). Choices and responses were made privately, allowing us to examine both source selection and subsequent information weighting without social pressure from the available sources.

Children and parents preferred to select each other over the other sources, and this preference did not change with child age (Fig. 3A-B; Table S5). How strongly children used social information remained largely independent of their chosen source, replicating Experiment 1 (Fig 3C). Among children, social information use declined with age (Table S6; β = -0.018, SE = 0.004, t(1,155) = -3.94, p < .001, 95% CI [-0.027, -0.009]), did not differ significantly between pathways, and this decline was slightly steeper for oblique than for vertical information (Table S6). Allowing participants to choose the source (Experiment 2), rather than assigning it (Experiment 1), did not change overall social information use (Table S7). Parents weighed social information from their own child more strongly than information from another child. At the mean child age, parents adjusted s = 0.207 toward estimates provided by their own child but significantly less toward estimates provided by another child (s = 0.145; β = -0.062, SE = 0.014, t(2,515) = -4.30, p < .001, 95% CI [-0.091, -0.034]; Fig. 3D; Table S6). Together with parents' strong preference to select their own child as an information source (Fig. 3B; Table S5), these findings provide evidence that vertical transmission can operate in both directions: parents not only preferentially selected their own child but also weighted that information more strongly than information from another child.

Complementary analyses. Our parent-child setup also allowed us to gauge several factors that might shape often-observed individual differences in social information use (56) (57) (58). Such differences could partly reflect stable tendencies shared across generations (potentially through transmission within families), but may also depend on immediate, situational features, such as the quality of one's own estimate and confidence in it. We therefore first tested the preregistered prediction of parent-child resemblance in social information use, and then examined associations with estimation accuracy and self-assessed task performance.

Social information use of children and parents do not resemble each other.

Across the full sample of 1,281 parent-child dyads, mean social information use was uncorrelated within dyads (Spearman's ρ = 0.04, p = .108; Fig. 4A). This absence of resemblance was consistent across the age range of the children (Fig. S1), indicating no increase in parent-child similarity over development. The same pattern was observed in the preregistered subset of Experiment 1 in which participants received social information from an unidentified source (Fig. S1A; Table S12), suggesting that the null association does not depend on participants knowing the identity of the information source.

Social information improved estimation accuracy, especially for children.

The availability of social information improved estimation accuracy for both children and parents (Fig. 4B-C). Estimation error (the proportional deviation from the true number of animals) decreased from the first to the second estimate across both Experiments 1 and 2 (β = -0.037, SE = 0.002, p < .001, 95% CI [-0.041, -0.033]). Children showed a substantially larger improvement in estimation accuracy than parents, with estimation error decreasing by about 6.1 percentage points among children compared with 1.6 percentage points among parents (interaction β = -0.045, SE = 0.004, p < .001, 95% CI [-0.052, -0.038]). Importantly, these gains were similar across transmission pathways (Fig. 4D): improvement was significant for vertical (Δ = 0.036), oblique (Δ = 0.039), and horizontal (Δ = 0.036) sources (all p < .001), and adding a source-byestimate interaction did not improve model fit (Table S8). Thus, social information improved estimation accuracy regardless of its source, with substantially larger accuracy gains among children than parents.

Self-rated performance predicts social information use, but only among parents does it track accuracy.

In a subset of sessions (N = 824 participants; 5,774 trials) participants rated their own performance relative to others, providing a task-level measure of confidence. We analyzed this exploratorily. Higher self-assessed performance predicted less social information use overall (β = -0.014, SE = 0.004, p < .001, 95% CI [-0.021, -0.006]), both among parents (p = .002) and children (p = .033; Fig. 4E; Table S9). However, higher self-assessed performance predicted greater estimation accuracy only among parents (β = -0.496, SE = 0.211, p = .020, 95% CI [-0.910, -0.081]); children's self-assessments was unrelated to their accuracy (p = .918; Fig. 4F; Table S9). Thus, participants' perceptions of their own performance predicted their reliance on social information in both generations, but accurately tracked performance only among parents.

Discussion

Despite the central role of vertical, oblique, and horizontal transmission in accounts of cultural change, it has remained unclear how strongly these pathways operate relative to one another when directly compared. Across two experiments using 1,281 parent-child dyads, we made this comparison while separating two components of social transmission: whom people choose to learn from, and how strongly they use social information once they have it. These two components behaved differently. Information weighting was largely indifferent to its source. Children and parents adjusted their estimates about as much toward a parent, an unrelated adult, or a peer, whether the source was assigned or freely chosen. By contrast, source selection was not indifferent. Given a free choice, children and parents overwhelmingly selected each other, on roughly two thirds of trials, and did so across the whole child-age range we studied. The predominance of vertical transmission in our data therefore arose mainly from source selection rather than information weighting. We also found evidence for transmission in the opposite generational direction, with adults incorporating information from children. This distinction between source selection and information weighting adds deeper insights into the behavioral mechanisms underlying transmission pathways studied in cultural evolutionary theory. Vertical, oblique, and horizontal transmission provide a useful abstraction for understanding how information moves through populations (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?MVSXMp but the strength of these pathways ultimately emerges from the behavior of individual learners. Our findings show that similar pathway-level patterns can arise from different underlying processes. In our study, differences between pathways arose almost entirely from source selection: when vertical, oblique, and horizontal sources were equally available, children and parents overwhelmingly selected their own family member, yet once information had been acquired, they weighted it similarly regardless of its source. Distinguishing these processes can therefore help explain not only which pathways dominate cultural transmission, but why they do so.

What drives the source-selection patterns remains an important question. In our study, vertical sources were familiar family members whereas alternative sources were generally unfamiliar, so the preference for vertical sources may reflect familiarity or other benefits of consulting a known person as well as the transmission pathway itself. Moreover, our oblique pathway was deliberately operationalized as an unfamiliar adult or child without any explicit cues to expertise, whereas real-world oblique transmission often involves familiar or knowledgeable individuals such as teachers and experts (4, 30, 59). Source preferences may also depend on what is being learned: developmental research shows that children selectively turn to different informants depending on characteristics such as their knowledge or expertise and the learning domain (30) (31) (32) (33). Notably, in our data, children's strong preference for their parent did not diminish with age, despite peers becoming increasingly prominent in children's social lives. This apparent contrast may partly reflect source availability: across development, children tend to spend less and less time with family and more time with peers (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?ftRDEl increasing opportunities for horizontal transmission. Greater peer influence in everyday life may therefore arise partly from greater opportunities for peer transmission, rather than from an increasing preference for peers when different sources are equally available. Future work could independently vary familiarity, expertise, and learning domain to establish which characteristics shape source selection.

The second clear pattern in our data was developmental. Children used more social information than parents at every age and in every transmission pathway. Parents' reliance was close to the level typically observed in adults, who tend to move only modestly toward social information (35, 55, 61). Children's reliance on social information declined steadily across middle childhood, consistent with accounts in which social learning is most valuable early in life, when personal knowledge is limited and uncertainty about one's own judgment is high (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?XSiaWk and with previous work documenting a decline in social information use from childhood into adulthood (34, 35, 64). This decline was somewhat weaker when information came from peers. Thus, peers did not become more influential in absolute terms, but their influence increased relative to other sources with age, consistent with the growing relative importance of peers over development (65) (66) (67). More broadly, development primarily changed how strongly children used social information rather than whom they chose to learn from: overall social information use declined across middle childhood, while the relative prominence of vertical transmission remained stable.

Parents' and other adults' behavior points to transmission from younger to older generations, an understudied phenomenon in the field of cultural evolution (16) (17) (18). Our findings provide experimental evidence that adults also learn from children, both vertically from their own child and obliquely from unrelated children. Importantly, participants made their judgments privately, so parents were under no social pressure to adjust their estimates toward those of their child or signal openness to do so. Rather, they voluntarily incorporated information provided by their own child, suggesting genuine reverse vertical transmission by integrating social information from their child into their judgments rather than public conformity. This reverse pathway could have consequences for how information spreads through populations. For example, innovations originating in younger generations may diffuse more rapidly when they are transmitted upward to parents, who can subsequently transmit them to other adults or to the next generation (68) (69) (70). Such reverse transmission may be particularly important during periods of rapid social or technological change, when younger generations encounter or adopt novel knowledge and practices before their parents do. In these circumstances, children can provide a route through which innovations spread from younger to older generations, potentially accelerating their diffusion beyond the cohort in which they first emerge (16, 18, 36, 71).

Our controlled design necessarily held several features of real-world transmission constant, providing directions for future work. First, the BEAST provides a reliable measure of social information use that correlates with behavior in other social-learning tasks, suggesting that it captures meaningful variation in people's broader propensity to use social information (34, 55). At the same time, the task used here involves estimates with an objectively correct answer.

Whether the same decomposition applies to domains without a single correct answer, such as norms, identity, or values, remains an important question, particularly as peers become increasingly influential during adolescence (27, 34, 47, 72). Second, extending the developmental range into adolescence would be particularly informative. Adolescence brings further changes in social environments and increasing opportunities for learning from peers and non-parental others (16https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?UqGkdr 19, (22) (23) (24) (25) (26) 45) , allowing tests of whether the stable source preferences observed across middle childhood persist as peer relationships become more prominent. Third, our comparison necessarily simplifies the diversity of relationships within each transmission pathway. Siblings are an especially important omission: depending on their relative ages, they may provide horizontal or age-structured social-learning opportunities and are both familiar and frequently available sources of information. Including siblings in an experimental design like the one we use here would therefore help establish how kinship, familiarity, age, and transmission pathway jointly shape source selection and information weighting. Finally, our sample was cross-sectional; longitudinal and network-based studies will be needed to trace how source selection and social information use change within individuals, and how information acquired by children subsequently spreads through families and wider social networks.

By comparing vertical, oblique, and horizontal transmission within parent-child dyads, this study suggests that transmission pathways are shaped less by how strongly social information is used than by whom learners choose to turn to. Across development, children became less receptive to social information, reducing how much was transmitted overall, while a stable preference for family members maintained the prominence of vertical transmission. Evidence for transmission from children to adults further shows that information can flow across generations in both directions, including within families. By providing a direct experimental comparison of the relative strength of the three transmission pathways, our findings contribute to a behavioral foundation for cultural evolutionary models, showing that transmission rates emerge from both source selection and information weighting. Understanding how these two processes vary across individuals, relationships, and environments may therefore be key to explaining how human cultures simultaneously preserve knowledge and remain capable of change.

Methods

Participants.

Participants were recruited at NEMO Science Museum in Amsterdam, The Netherlands, and invited to complete a brief experiment. All participants were fluent in Dutch or English. Sessions lasted about 15 minutes and took place in a dedicated testing space in the museum. Participants were instructed not to communicate with each other during the session. Data were collected across 10 waves between 2021 and 2025. The two experiments shared the same core task but differed in whether the information source was assigned (Experiment 1) or chosen (Experiment 2); for additional variations in terms of procedure, see below. The study was approved by the Ethics Review Board of the University of Amsterdam (2021-DP-13852).

After excluding sessions involving task-relevant communication, insufficient task comprehension, or participants who were not a parent-child dyad, we excluded participants younger than 6 years (who generally showed poor understanding) or with missing age, as well as those with unusually poor performance (mean absolute error exceeding 3 median absolute deviations from the sample median). Furthermore, as preregistered, we excluded trials in which participants adjusted away from the social information or beyond the observed estimate (corresponding to s < 0 or s > 1; see Task below). For the parent-child resemblance analysis (Fig. 4A; Fig. S1), we additionally required at least three trials with adjustments within the0 ≤ s ≤ 1 range per participant. The final sample consisted of 1,281 parent-child dyads, including 1,262 with children aged 6-13 years and 19 with children aged 14-18 years (children: mean age = 9.0 years, SD = 2.0; parents: mean age = 41.9 years, SD = 5.42). The 19 dyads with children aged 14-18 years were retained in the statistical analyses to avoid unnecessarily excluding valid observations, but omitted from figures focusing on middle childhood. Because exclusions were applied to each participant individually, the number of parents and children differs slightly in analyses that do not require both dyad members. Experiment 1 was preregistered on the Open Science Framework (https://osf.io/9xn87). Experiment 2 was not, and all associated analyses are exploratory. The preregistration specifies hypotheses for a subset of the data collection that we later extended. We report the full correspondence between the preregistered analyses and those presented in this paper, including the preregistered results in their original form, in Table S1.

Task. Social information use was measured with the Berlin Estimate Adjustment Task (Fig. 1). Participants completed the task privately on their own tablet. On each trial, participants viewed an image of a group of animals (between 50 and 100) for 6 seconds and entered an initial estimate of the number of animals shown (E₁) on a slider ranging from 0 to 150, initialized at 0. Participants had to move the slider before they could continue. Participants were instructed to estimate as accurately as possible. After entering their first estimate, they observed social information in the form of another participant's estimate (X), displayed alongside their own first estimate, and entered a second estimate (E₂).

No performance feedback was provided between trials. Trial-level social information use was quantified as the relative adjustment toward the social information, s = (E₂ -E₁) / (X -E₁), where s = 0 indicates no adjustment and s = 1 indicates adoption of the observed estimate. Each participant's overall social information use (S) was defined as their mean s across included trials. The experiment was programmed in the LIONESS Lab (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?FC0lXA) and administered on Samsung Galaxy Tab S7 tablets (12.4 inch) running the Fully Kiosk Browser app (version 1.56.2-play). For the data collection protocol and screenshots of all experimental materials, see Experimental Materials in the Supplementary Information.

Design. The study comprised two experiments contrasting three transmission pathways: vertical (between parent and child), oblique (between a child and an unrelated adult, or a parent and unrelated child), and horizontal (between same-aged peers; Fig. 1). The oblique pathway was operationalized without cues to expertise or prestige. In both experiments, all responses (source choices and estimates) were entered privately on each participant's own tablet, so that a co-present family member could not observe or influence them. Experiment 1 (preregistered) assigned the source of social information, in a between-subjects design. Parent-child dyads completed the task independently, although two dyads could be tested simultaneously in the same testing space. Vertical social information was generated live by the participant's co-present family member, so participants saw an estimate their parent or child had just entered. Oblique and horizontal social information consisted of estimates from different participants who had completed the task previously. Child sources were matched to the age of the child in the focal dyad, whereas adult sources were drawn from previous adult participants.

To keep the informational value of social information comparable across sources, prerecorded estimates were selected to fall in the direction of the true value (T), approximately 20% away from the participant's own estimate (55). So, target values were set near 1.2 × E₁ when E₁ < T and near 0.8 × E₁ when E₁ > T, with the closest available prerecorded estimate selected subject to the relevant source constraint. Oblique and horizontal social information was thus controlled, whereas vertical social information was not. For children, social information use (s) did not vary systematically with the distance between the first estimate and the social information, whereas parents weighted more distant social information less strongly (Fig. S3). Thus, distancedependent weighting is unlikely to account for our main developmental results.

Experiment 2 allowed participants to choose their source separately on each trial from three options: Parent (vertical), Adult (oblique), or Peer (horizontal). The experiment was conducted in two slightly different setups. In waves 7-8, parent-child dyads formed separate experimental units. Vertical social information was generated live by the co-present family member, whereas oblique and horizontal information came from prerecorded estimates of previous participants, calibrated as in Experiment 1. In waves 9-10, two parent-child dyads participated simultaneously, forming a single experimental unit and allowing information from all three source types to be generated live by co-present participants. Results were similar across these setups, supporting their inclusion in the same analyses. Information weighting showed little evidence of differences (Table S10), while the physical presence of another family was associated with a slight increase in their selection, mostly in oblique transmission (children +5.1 percentage points (pp), adults +6.0 pp; Fig. S4, Table S11).

In wave 2, participants completed five additional trials in which the source of the social information was not identified as a child or adult. These "source-neutral" trials closely followed the original BEAST procedure (55) and were included to test parent-child resemblance in social information use independently of source identity. The preregistered analysis shown in Fig. S1 uses these trials only; to increase statistical power the analysis in Fig. 4A extends the analysis to all available dyads from both experiments, using each participant's mean S across included trials.

Procedure. Participants entered their age and gender on the tablet before beginning the task, then completed the task seated near their parent or child. Seating arrangements varied somewhat across data collection waves because of changes in museum facilities (e.g., face-toface, facing away, or separated by a divider). Because all responses were entered privately on individual tablets, participants could not observe one another's decisions, and analyses indicated no meaningful effect of seating arrangement on social information use (Table S10). Furthermore, in a subset of waves (waves 2-5), we administered a post-task measure of self-assessed performance, asking participants to place themselves on a ladder ranging from people who were very poor to very good at the estimation task (Fig. 4E-F).

Statistical analysis.

Analyses were conducted in R (version 4.6.0). Linear mixed-effects models were fitted with lme4 (version 2.0-1) and lmerTest (version 3.2-1), multinomial mixedeffects models with mclogit (version 0.9.15), post hoc contrasts computed with emmeans (version 2.0.3), and model R² values with MuMIn (version 1.48.19). Experimental data and analysis code can be found in the repository associated with this paper.

The same linear mixed-effects models were used in both experiments, so that estimates of information weighting are directly comparable across the assigned-source and chosen-source designs (Table S7). These models tested whether social information use differed between children and parents, declined with child age (mean-centered), and varied across transmission pathways, including the participant type × pathway interaction, with post hoc pairwise contrasts from estimated marginal means comparing pathways within children and within parents. An exploratory model additionally included age (continuous) and receiver group (child vs. parent) and their interactions with transmission pathway (Fig. 2; Table S4).

In Experiment 2, the analyses separated the two components of transmission. Source selection, that is, whose estimate participants chose to observe, was modeled with mixed multinomial logistic regression predicting the chosen source (vertical, oblique, or horizontal) based on age, fitted separately for children and parents, each using participant as random intercept (Table S5). Information weighting was modeled as in Experiment 1, with linear mixed-effects models of triallevel adjustment as a function of age and chosen source, fitted separately for children and parents (Fig. 3C-D; Table S6).

Parent-child resemblance in social information use was assessed with Spearman rank correlations between the mean adjustment (S) of the parent and the child in each dyad (Fig. 4A). The preregistered analysis, restricted to the (source-neutral) trials from wave 2, is reported in Fig. S1.

As exploratory analyses, we examined estimation accuracy, defined as the proportional error of each estimate relative to the true count, before and after exposure to social information and as a function of source (Fig. 4B-D; Table S8). Further exploratory analyses of self-rated confidence are reported in Fig. 4E-F and Table S9. Fig. S3. Social information use (s) as a function of the relative distance between the first estimate and the social information, |X -E1| / E1, for children (left) and parents (right). These data focus on "live" sources only (which were present in the room and thus generated endogenous social information): vertical trials from all waves, and all sources from waves 9-10 (Methods). Dots show individual trials; the line is a LOESS fit with a 95% confidence band. The dashed vertical line marks the 20% target at which prerecorded oblique and horizontal sources were fixed in waves 1-8 (i.e., where controlled social information was located). Horizontal banding at s = 0, 0.5, and 1 reflects the coarse adjustments that were possible when the first estimate and the social information are only a few units apart. The x-axis is truncated at the 95th percentile of distance for display; all trials were included in the models. For children, social information use showed little systematic relationship with distance (slope = 0.000, 95% CI [-0.011, 0.012], p = .97). Parents showed distance discounting, weighting social information less strongly as its distance from their initial estimate increased (slope = -0.217, 95% CI [-0.248, -0.186], p < .001), and the decline was non-linear (quadratic over linear: χ²(2) = 72.0, p < .001). This discounting is consistent with previous findings (https://www.zotero.org/google-docs/?npYyA9.

Fig. S4. Source selection and information use in children tested with one versus two parent-child dyads present (Experiment 2). A-B, Proportion of trials in which children selected vertical (red), oblique (green), or horizontal (blue) sources, plotted against child age. A, one parent-child dyad present; B, two parentchild dyads present. Sample sizes (distinct participants) are shown above each bar. C-D, Mean social information use (S) following source selection, plotted against child age. C, one dyad present; D, two dyads present. Symbols represent per-age means, with symbol size proportional to sample size. Lines show linear mixed-effects model predictions with 95% confidence intervals.
