3. Experimental Materials

Translations below are based on their Dutch originals.

Oral instructions protocol.

At the start of each session, the experimenter communicated the following points in the order shown. The wording did not need to be verbatim: experimenters presented the instructions naturally and in language appropriate for children aged six years and older. At least one parent of each participating child was present during the instructions.

1. Welcome and general introduction. "Welcome! You are going to take part in an experiment about decision-making from the Department of Psychology at the University of Amsterdam. We cannot tell you exactly what the experiment is about beforehand, but we will explain this afterwards." 2. Information and voluntary participation. "Before we start, you will receive this information letter. The most important things to know are that your data will be anonymous (we cannot tell who you are from your data) and that you can stop participating at any time." → Hand out the tablets.

3. Explanation of the task.

"There is a short task on the tablet, and you will also see instructions on the screen. You will see pictures with animals and your task is to estimate as accurately as possible how many animals there are, using a slider. After making your first estimate, you will see another person's estimate. You can then make a second estimate. The other person saw the same picture and was also asked to estimate the number of animals as accurately as possible."

In waves 1-8, when estimates from previous participants could be shown, we added: "Some of these estimates come from people who previously visited the museum and completed this task. We stored their answers so that we can show them to you."

4. Source choice (Experiment 2 only).

"In each round, you can choose whose estimate you want to see: your own parent [for parents: your own child], another adult, or another child."

In waves 7-8 we added: "The other adult and child completed the task previously."

In waves 9-10 we added "the other adult and child are the members of the other family participating now with you in the same session". This screenshot shows the instructions for Experiment 2, in which participants chose whose estimate they wanted to see. The three other players had been explicitly introduced during the oral instructions (see above): for children, their own parent, another adult, and another child. For parents, "your parent" was replaced by "your child."

In Experiment 1, the source was assigned. The screen specified that participants would see the estimate of their own parent/child in the vertical condition, or of another adult/child who had completed the task previously in the oblique/horizontal conditions, consistent with the preceding oral instructions. The screen read: "After entering your estimate, you will see the estimate of [source]. Then you can make a second estimate of how many animals there were. There are five rounds in total." In the oblique and horizontal conditions, this was followed by: "This task has been completed by many museum visitors. In each round you will see the estimate of a different person."

After this screen, the task started.

At the start of each round, the participants observed an image with a group of animals. This screenshot shows the stimulus presented in the first round. The five rounds always presented the same stimuli in a fixed order: 93 ladybugs, 78 dragonflies, 59 butterflies, 74 bees, and 69 stag beetles.

The slider was initialized at 0, and participants could not continue without moving it. The number displayed above the slider updated as the slider was moved.

This screen was shown only in Experiment 2, in which participants selected the source. In Experiment 1, the source was assigned. For parents, the top left screen shows a smaller icon with "My child" (see next screenshot).

For parents, "My parent" was replaced by "My child"; all other source options were unchanged. Like the previous screen, this screen was only shown in Experiment 2.

Participants then entered a second estimate using the same slider. The social information (the other person's estimate) was shown in red above the slider.

At the end of the task, participants received feedback on the accuracy of their first and second estimates and their social information use. This feedback was provided to encourage discussion between children and their parents about social influence, as required by the museum.

For "Source selection shapes the strength of cultural transmission pathways" by Sherilyn Segura, Wouter van den Bos, Andrea Gradassi, Esra Cemre Su de Groot, Lucia Hendrikse, Lieke Hofmans, Fleur Korzilius, Sheina Lew-Levy, Ana da Silva Pinho, Scarlett Slagter, Sjanne van der Stappen, Lucas Molleman

Contents 1. Supplementary Figures 2. Supplementary Tables 3. Experimental Materials 4. Supplementary References 1. Supplementary Figures S12. A. The preregistered analysis, restricted to Wave 2, including trials in which social information came from anonymous previous participants (n = 236). B-D. The full sample across all waves, split by child age: B. 6 to 8 years, C. 9 to 11 years, D. 12 to 18 years. The main-text analysis (Fig. 4A) pools all dyads (n = 1,281; ρ = 0.04, p = .108). No subset showed a meaningful parent-child correlation under either method.

The one nominally significant value (panel C, ρ = 0.08, p = .044) is very small in magnitude, is not significant under Pearson correlation (r = 0.06, p = .146), and does not survive correction for the three age bands tested. Together, these analyses provide little evidence for meaningful parent-offspring resemblance across sources, waves, correlation methods, or child age. See Table S12 for statistical details.

After each round, the task returned to the stimulus screen shown above and the same sequence was repeated with the next animal species, for five rounds in total.

Fig 1.

Fig 1. Experimental setup and transmission pathways. A. Trial structure of the Berlin Estimate Adjustment Task (BEAST (55)). On each trial, participants viewed an image containing 50-100 animals for 6 seconds and entered a first estimate (E₁) by positioning a slider. They then observed the estimate of another participant (X), displayed together with their own first estimate (E₁) on the slider, before entering a second estimate (E₂). In Experiment 1, the social information source was assigned; in Experiment 2, participants chose their source. B. Social information use was quantified as s = (E₂ -E₁) / (X -E₁), capturing the relative extent of moving one's estimate toward the social information on a given trial. Each participant's mean adjustment across trials is denoted S. C. The three transmission pathways. In the vertical pathway, children received the estimate of their own parent (SV), while parents received that of their child (SRV, where R in the subscript denotes "reverse"). In the oblique pathway, children received an estimate from an unrelated adult (SO); parents received an estimate from an unrelated child (SRO). In the horizontal pathway, participants received the estimate from another individual of the same generation (SH).

Fig 3.

Fig 3. Source selection and information use across development (Experiment 2). A-B, Proportion of trials in which participants selected vertical (red), oblique (green), or horizontal (blue) sources, plotted against child age. A, children; B, parents. Sample sizes (distinct participants) are shown above each bar. C-D, Mean social information use (S) following source selection, plotted against child age. Symbols represent per-age means, with symbol size proportional to sample size. Lines show linear mixed-effects model predictions with 95% confidence intervals. The x-axis corresponds to the age of the child in each dyad; parent data are plotted at their child's age. The dotted blue line in D indicates adults' mean social information use when receiving information from another adult (horizontal transmission), for which childage is not applicable.

Fig 4.

Fig 4. Parent-child resemblance, estimation accuracy, and self-rated performance. A. Children's social information use (Schild) plotted against their parents' social information use (Sparent) across 1,281 parent-child dyads, where S denotes each participant's mean adjustment toward social information across trials (Fig 1). Each point represents one dyad; the line shows the fitted linear regression with 95% confidence intervals. B-C, Mean proportional estimation error (lower values indicate greater accuracy) before (E₁, dashed) and after (E₂, solid) incorporating social information, plotted against age for children (B) and parents (C). Shaded bands show 95% confidence intervals around LOESS smoothers. D. Mean proportional estimation error before and after social information by transmission pathway, shown separately for children (red) and parents (blue). Numerical labels indicate mean estimation error (see Fig S2 for breakdown by experiment). E-F, Associations between self-rated performance and social information use (E) and first-estimate accuracy (F) for children (red) and parents (blue). Lines show fitted regressions with 95% confidence intervals.

Fig S2.

Fig S2. Estimation accuracy before and after social information by transmission pathway and experiment. Mean proportional estimation error is shown for children (A, red) and parents (B, blue). Gray points indicate error before social information; colored open circles indicate error after social information. Numerical labels give the corresponding means; lower values indicate greater accuracy. V denotes vertical transmission pooled across Experiments 1 and 2. O1 and O2 denote oblique transmission in Experiments 1 and 2, respectively; H1 and H2 denote horizontal transmission in Experiments 1 and 2, respectively.

z = 0.46, p = 1.000; V-H z = 1.90, p = .173 Main text; Fig.2;

Oblique vs. vertical b = -0.062, p < .001 Main text; Fig.3D;

5 . 6.

No communication during the task. "It is important that during the experiment you do not talk or communicate with the other participants in the room. If you have a question during the experiment, please ask one of us." Start task. "You can now start. You will see the most important things about the task again on the screen."
